#7243. Can ad tech’s server-side shift save it from Apple and Google?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Apple has reminded ad tech how much power the browser holds. Ad tech’s reaction: Get out of the browser.

With iOS 27, Apple’s Safari browser has begun blocking domains tied to identity resolution providers, such as The Trade Desk and ID5, that link people’s browsing to their email addresses. In response, ad tech firms and IAB Tech Lab are pushing to move ad calls server-side, including through the latter organization’s Trusted Server technology, which would limit the browser’s control over ad tech firms’ actions.

Meanwhile, the remedies ruling in Google’s ad tech antitrust trial has given Prebid an opening to flip the programmatic auction. Instead of Google’s ad server getting final say on the winning bid, Google’s ad exchange would have to bid into Prebid’s server.

But is the server-side shift a long-term solution? And is it the right one? Digiday’s executive editor of news Seb Joseph and senior ad tech reporter Ronan Shields joined the Digiday Podcast to hash out ad tech’s Game of Thrones amid the server-side shift.

Joseph: From the conversations we’re having in the initial aftermath of the Safari crackdown, Trusted Server has been seen as almost a panacea. And yet it does feel like that is a misappropriation of what Trusted Server was designed for, and that any attempt to truly lean on it as a durable workaround to what Apple is doing misses the point entirely. It’s pretty clear that Apple is not going to stop at the companies that it has already targeted and, more importantly, the mechanism that they use to do that. Whether it’s happening via Safari or via Trusted Server, Apple does not want that stuff happening to its customers in any guise and will by hook or crook find a way to offset that.

Shields: Can ad tech wrest back control from these power brokers of Apple and Google? I would say absolutely no. They can’t wrest control because Google and Apple own the browser, the mobile OS — in the case of Google — they own the ad tech platform and the cloud infrastructure as well. With Trusted Server, [ad tech companies] can gain a degree of autonomy, but they need to behave themselves with it to retain that autonomy.

Joseph: Is it incumbent on the industry at some point to get its shit together and do more than the bare minimum, the baseline set by regulators, in order to instill some sort of trust? Because otherwise the browsers, the platforms, they will find a way to put their hand on the scale moving forward.

Shields: I hear what you’re saying, Seb, but how realistic is that?

Joseph: I’d say it was pretty realistic this time around, because otherwise what happens?

Shields: If we listen to [Google’s] earnings yesterday [Oct. 28], the one aspect of their business that didn’t do well again is this [third-party display ad] network business, the GAM area of the business. I think they’re OK with letting it go. So Prebid wants it? Yeah, fine, go ahead. I don’t think they’re going to lose too much sleep over it.

#7239. Agency execs call for retail media to be holistically considered beyond the lower funnel

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

As it continues to expand in the number of channels and options, as well as the ad revenue placed into it, retail media is offering media agencies new opportunities to harness closed-loop measurement in a way that benefits both upper- and lower-funnel marketing efforts.

That’s one major takeaway from a conversation onstage Dentsu’s president of commerce, North America, Aisha Khan, at Digiday’s Media Buying Summit last week in Newport Beach, Calif., last week. Khan said she also believes that the in-store side of retail media has great upward potential.

“There’s still tremendous growth to be had in the commerce landscape,” Khan told the audience, citing the stat that more than 80% of sales are still happening in store, but less than 5% of retail media is spent on in-store media. “So if you think about that expansion opportunity for retailers to really digitize the physical experience, that is going to create tremendous tremendous growth for retail media — think about that last point of purchase.”

In store may also be a way to bring down the expense of reaching consumers at the lower-end of the purchase funnel. “Retail media is very effective, right? It’s at one of the lowest points of in the funnel, and but it’s very expensive, so the efficiency is not quite there,” said Kahn. “So I think in a good way there’ll be a bit more balance between the effectiveness and the efficiency, which will help to actually grow the next phase of commerce media, so I think while some areas will be will come down or plateau, there will be other areas of growth like the in-store piece.”

And in an earlier session on Day 2 of the Media Buying Summit, Instrument CEO Laurel Burton talked about how all the distraction around AEO and GEO has made the industry overlook the value of websites as part of an overall commerce strategy when, to her mind, the opposite is true.

“My prediction is the exact opposite that websites are dying,” Burton told the audience at a session on the melding of creative and tech with media. “I think they are the absolute central hub that needs to create a consistent experience because they’re feeding both that consumer and also that lanswer engine. You literally can’t be a product and a brand without this as a channel.”

Burton’s fellow speaker, Crocs CMO Carly Gomez (who happens to be an Instrument client), elaborated on Burton’s position saying the website needs to be coordinated in its messaging with other marketing channels.

“As people are discovering your brand, yes, they may come in through an LLM,” Gomez said. “But then if they want more information on your brand, their next stop either is your social media site or one of your social media handles or your e-commerce or your app. And essentially you want them all to feel cohesive and deliver the best possible experience.”

And yet, the upper-funnel brand-building side of retail media can’t be overlooked, argued Dentsu’s Khan, who pointed to the likes of CVS and Kroger as two retail media network providers who are helping to push the creative boundaries further (but didn’t elaborate).

“It goes back to ensuring that you’re getting the incrementality and the efficiency out of it, and that it’s worth the extra dollars that you’re paying for the closed-loop measurement,” she said. “People forget that there’s so much opportunity to come back to the power of creative in retail media, and I think retail media has been relegated to this performance marketing channel. But if you think about it, with the in-store and the shopper marketing experience, you still need that unmissable creative component too.”

Having just also picked up Diageo’s shopper marketing business, Khan noted that AI-powered Media Mix Modeling (MMM) will also help bring down the cost, even as consolidation will likely reduce the total number of retail media networks out there.

“Retail media’s boom was based on the back of closed loop measurement — this promise that you could understand with every dollar what you’re actually driving on the other side,” said Khan. “But I also believe AI has accelerated this idea of of agile MMM, or incrementality — more traditional MMM becoming more sophisticated. And so, as you think about closed-loop measurement, advertisers are investing in their own capabilities to invest across retailers and get that closed loop measurement themselves, instead of having the retailers grade their own homework. What happens is that data CPM that retailers have been charging for closed-loop measurement potentially does compress.”

#7238. Where agency strategists see opportunity as AI challenges their worth

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While strategists fear the tech will replace their roles, the majority agree that there are also reasons to be optimistic, according to a WARC report.

The advertising industry has faced significant challenges this year, marked by agency consolidations at major holding companies and sweeping headcount reductions. Between August 2025 and August 2026, the industry recorded a loss of 6,900 jobs, per Emarketer.

While there are many factors behind the cuts, one of the most prominent has been the rise of artificial intelligence across all aspects of the industry. From data analysis to campaign generation, AI’s adoption has been swift across nearly all marketing disciplines, leaving many executives apprehensive about the future.

Still, 66% of agency-side strategists agree there are clear reasons to be optimistic, according to WARC’s Future of Strategy 2026 report.

“We’re not saying the problems are solved, but we are saying the tides may be turning,” said Lena Roland, content director at WARC Strategy. “After a period of layoffs and consolidation, there’s a sense that things might be stabilizing and strategists can [begin] to look forward again.”

The reasons for the guarded positivity are simple: Clients are seeing a need for thinking that is rooted in human insights, Roland explained. Strategy influence with clients jumped to 50%, up from 41% in 2025, per WARC.

“This indicates that clients are increasingly recognizing the value of strategy, particularly at a time when growth is harder to achieve,” Roland said. “AI can help us understand the world as it is; strategists can help us understand what it can become.”

Ironically, AI is helping fuel strategists’ confidence. Nearly two-thirds, 65%, of the 489 agency-side strategists surveyed said AI gives them time for more nuanced, strategic work. Simultaneously, 55% believe the tech will refocus strategy on human perspectives. WARC’s report was fielded in August.

“Strategists are positioned as the best people to be guardians of reality by grounding ideas in real human insights,” Roland said. “In an age of AI, humanity is a competitive advantage. AI can’t replace human judgment or intuition, and it can’t ask the questions that are really worth asking.”

Not yet, anyway. Despite their optimism for the near-term, strategists are more wary about the long-term. More than half of the strategists polled in WARC’s study — 56%, up from 41% last year — are concerned that AI will eventually advance enough to replace them.

“They’re more optimistic about the future, but they’re also realistic about the scale of change,” Roland said. “The strategists that will thrive are those who learn to use AI well, while still bringing the human perspective, critical thinking and commercial understanding that makes their work valuable.”

However, the technology could become a double-edged sword: The same tools that strategists are using to move upstream with clients could also be leveraged by account executives, explained Michael Farmer, chairman and CEO at marketing consultancy Farmer & Company.

“When agencies talk about AI [right now], they’re not talking about how they can do a more strategic job for their clients,” Farmer said. “If they have the will, the senior account executives can [use AI] to offer more strategy.”

Doing so would elevate account executives to more of a business partner role, rather than one more akin to a traffic manager whose job is to get stuff out the door, Farmer said. He explained that over the past 30-some years, the account executive role has been diminished through the rise of programmatic negotiating, the unbundling of media and creative and the introduction of strategists in the late ‘90s and early 2000s.

“They took away a responsibility that the account people already had,” Farmer said. “They used to tell me when I first got started that the strategist was ‘the conscience of the consumer’ … They were there to kind of provide discipline between caring about the brand and caring about the brief that they got from the client.”

Often, that meant rewriting the briefs accordingly, Farmer said. Now, AI can often write those briefs as well — if not better — than a strategist can, which is reflected in the sentiment from strategists that they have more time to think beyond the brief. Agencies and their strategists will need to find how to prove their value beyond their traditional remit.

“Strategy as a discipline has too often been treated as a supporting function, or even thrown in for free, rather than recognized as a valuable service in its own right,” Roland said. “There’s an opportunity now for strategists to become genuine growth partners, helping shape not just how a brand communicates, but where the business goes next.”

Fortunately for strategists, many are already aware of the need to prove business results. In WARC’s survey, 80% of strategists said they must develop stronger evidence to prove their business impact, and 57% said they need a new value proposition and compensation model.

“AI is a great opportunity, but you have to be a strategist to realize that it could help you tap that unrealized potential,” Farmer said.

Which is ultimately why strategists see hope in the face of AI’s headwinds. Businesses will always be under pressure to demonstrate growth and will always need people who can find new ways to achieve it, whether that is on their own or with the help of AI, Roland explained.

“Businesses will always need people who can diagnose and solve problems,” Roland said. “The best strategists will use AI to get beyond the obvious and connect creativity to commercial outcomes, helping businesses see possibilities they couldn’t see before.”

Editor’s note: Informa, which owns a controlling stake in Marketing Dive’s publisher Informa TechTarget, is also invested in WARC. Informa has no influence over Marketing Dive’s coverage.

#7236. Ad Tech Briefing: Google’s AI-reckoning

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Ad Tech Briefing covers the latest in ad tech and platforms for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Tuesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

It’s earnings season, typically the time of year when industry observers watch agog, as Big Tech companies post earnings far greater than the average ad tech company can begin to contemplate, but Q3 this year has thrown us a curveball to the usual narrative.

The unexpected glitch in the latest round ran contrary to the expected narrative, i.e., Google’s punishment for the success of its AI, a testament to the extent of the harsh realities facing the digital media sector as a whole when it comes to sustaining revenue.

It’s also likely to serve as an important footnote in the future filings of Google’s legal department, with tuned-in observers likely to recall how it was able to cite rapid AI advancements as proof of a fiercely competitive and evolving tech landscape.

Even if Google did lose both trials (on paper, at least) last year, it can be argued the above argument was made successfully to justify it continuing without forced divestiture, and in Q3 (it would appear) this argument impacted the biggest company in advertising’s bottom line.

Since then, Google has spent much of the year telling advertisers and investors that AI will make its search business bigger, not smaller. However, last week’s earnings offered the clearest indication yet that delivering on that promise is proving complicated.

On Oct. 28, Google’s parent company Alphabet reported that its search queries were growing at their fastest annual rate in five years, with total Q3 revenues nearing $127.6 billion, up 25% year-on-year.

However, a deeper analysis reveals a more troubling trend for the hegemon of Madison Avenue: more people are searching, but Google’s AI-driven search experiences aren’t yet generating ad revenue at the same rate as conventional search.

However, revenue from Google’s “Search & other” operations increased 12% to $63.35 billion, with Google’s chief business officer Philipp Schindler acknowledging as much during the company’s third-quarter earnings call, telling analysts that AI Mode’s comparatively limited monetization had contributed to the slowdown.

The disclosure is significant because search remains Google’s biggest financial engine and supports its (increasingly expensive) AI ambitions.

Advertising revenue, i.e., search in combination with other areas of its media operations, such as “YouTube” (Q3 revenues of $11.2 billion) and “Network” (Q3 revenues of $7.45 billion) — that’s the ad tech side, which is continuing its prolonged decline in revenue — accounts for approximately 64% of Alphabet’s revenue, down from 72% a year earlier.

More queries, less revenue per opportunity

Wall Street was unimpressed. Alphabet shares fell approximately 12.6% on Oct. 29, erasing close to $500 billion in market value, as investors questioned the economics of the company’s AI transition with coverage in the immediate aftermath of the results highlighting disappointment in Google’s revenue performance.

Google maintains that AI is expanding search activity rather than merely replacing existing queries. Sundar Pichai, CEO, Alphabet, told analysts that AI Mode queries are, on average, three times longer than traditional searches, while approximately one in five involves voice, images or documents.

However, executives also acknowledged that AI Mode currently monetizes at a lower rate, and further hinted that it is now expanding advertising within AI Mode internationally and introducing multi-turn advertisements in the U.S., allowing commercial placements to appear during extended conversations.

Meanwhile, the continued struggles of “Google Network” revenues, i.e., ad tech, is likely to stimulate further grimacing from publishers, with this unit generating a modest 1.4% gain in revenues during the period, with this trend accompanied by an overall increase in traffic acquisition costs, or “TAC,” of approximately 10% during the period.

Overall, TAC hit $16.4 billion, with the figures highlighting how higher impression prices can mask shrinking volumes as publishers contend with declining search referrals and unpredictable traffic. This creates an awkward backdrop for Google’s court-ordered ad tech remedies. Prebid’s proposed auction overhaul could improve publisher control and interoperability, but neither can restore lost audiences or guarantee advertiser demand.

“Can we really blame Apple for blocking ID tech?”

#7235. How the sausage is made: Inside Johnsonville’s political parody campaign

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A campaign that culminated with the midterm elections sought to engage Americans around a distaste for politics while avoiding partisanship.

On Nov. 2, Johnsonville gave out 1,000 $20 promotional codes that will cover the ingredients for a sausage dinner. The brand also set up shop in Rosa Parks Circle in Grand Rapids, Michigan, the epicenter of a hotly contested Senate race, to dole out free food and product vouchers.

The reason for the sausage fest was simple: Johnsonville’s research shows that consumers are sometimes so consumed by politics — like on the eve of a contentious election day — that they forget to make plans for dinner.

In an environment where Americans rank politics and government as the most important problems facing the country, according to Gallup research, most brand marketers have steered clear of politics, to say nothing of brands that have been dragged into the political muck against their will.

That’s not the case for Johnsonville, the Wisconsin-based sausage brand that launched a campaign in September parodying the tropes of political advertising, suggesting that Americans choose “Sausage for Dinner” in 2026. The effort includes a TV spot, events, an Amazon Ads collaboration and the kind of yard signs and merchandise used by political candidates, with local billboard ads calling for “Affordable Protein for the People” and “A Sausage in Every Air Fryer.”

“This idea is really about parodying the genre and making something fun that stands out amongst all of the dark negative stuff that's just bombarding all of us,” said Jamie Schmelzer, vice president of marketing at Johnsonville.

No matter how politically polarized the country has become, a distaste for politics unites Americans. More than eight in 10 agree that society has become too political, about three-quarters say they hate political advertising and 84% think their community could make better use of the billions spent on political ads this year, per Harris Poll data commissioned by Johnsonville.

“This campaign is talking about politics, but it's not saying anything partisan,” said Schmelzer. “We're all going through this together, regardless of whether you're left, right or independent.”

Or, as Rob Riggle says in a PSA-style campaign video in which the comedian portrays a sausage envoy: “Sausage will unite the mild Italians with the Cajuns, the Mexicans and the Polishes in a glorious crockpot of freedom that will make your tongue burn red, white and blue."

Along with feelings on politics, Johnsonville’s Harris Poll data revealed an insight around how people are constantly struggling to think of what to eat for dinner. The stress caused by politics and current events is only exacerbating that struggle, with more than one-third of Americans saying they were so consumed by current events at least once in the last year that they forgot to figure out what to have for dinner.

“It [was] going to get even noisier, right on top of back to school time, when everybody's getting back in the routine of figuring out what to put on the table for dinner every night, amidst activities and all the other things that come with that time of year,” Schmelzer said about the campaign’s launch timing. “It felt like the right way for our brand to help out by just providing ideas for dinner.”

To help make dinner planning easier, Johnsonville teamed up with Amazon Ads Brand Innovation Lab as part of the campaign. The brand and platform rolled out a custom experience that let consumers browse 30-minute recipes and add ingredients directly to their Amazon Grocery carts. The Nov. 2 promotional code push was also part of the Amazon tie-up.

“For us, [Amazon’s] ability to get everything you need to make dinner on your doorstep quickly was great, and for them, making sure that Americans know that if they need an idea in a pinch, Amazon has the capability,” Schmelzer said.

The “Sausage for Dinner” campaign also includes a purpose-driven partnership with Hunger Free America, with Johnsonville pledging $100,000 to fight food insecurity and the first 100 customers to join the cause receiving a campaign swag bag. The Amazon collaboration saw the e-commerce giant donate $1 to the organization for every hour of eligible viewing of a curated collection of comedies — including “Black Sheep,” “Head of State” and “A League of Their Own” — available through Fire TV.

“We thought a charitable component was really important to this. Groceries are expensive, and that is not a partisan perspective, either. It's affecting everybody,” Schmelzer said. “Sometimes it's about not having the idea [for dinner], but for some people, it's also a financial challenge, and so we wanted to do what we could to help.”

#7232. Here are the questions on advertisers’ minds after the DoorDash-Instacart deal

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

It’s been almost a week since DoorDash officially announced its plans to acquire Instacart — a deal valued at approximately $17 billion.

DoorDash’s takeover of Instacart isn’t expected to wrap until the end of 2027 at the earliest or the first half of 2028 at the latest, but media buyers are already questioning what this could mean for the retail media landscape.

“In the short term, I don’t expect this to meaningfully disrupt the retail media landscape. But longer term, the potential is significant,” said Nik Hengel, vp of analytics at New Engen digital marketing agency.

The deal still has to be approved by the Federal Trade Commission (FTC) and not flagged as an antitrust violation. In the meantime, however, media buyers are more concerned with costs, consolidation and capabilities.

Here are five questions media buyers and industry analysts have about the pending acquisition:

Advertisers are salivating at what these two networks could offer them — from DoorDash’s delivery network of 993 million total orders, per DoorDash’s Q3 earnings and Instacart’s grocery and fulfillment technology (as well as its 91.4 million orders, per Instacart’s Q3 earnings).

The joint venture could also mean a single ecosystem with closed-loop data reporting and a scaled ads platform.

“But bigger platforms don’t automatically mean simplicity,” said Bill Wise, CEO and co-founder of Mediaocean, an adtech company. “Brands still have to manage their investments across multiple ecosystems, each with its own data, rules, and measurement.”

However, the two already have separate ad businesses — DoorDash Ads and Instacart Ads — with their own tech offerings. The real test will be how the two delivery platforms will integrate the advertising components of their business without upending how advertisers buy media.

Scott Paternoster, CEO of performance-marketing agency Chief Media, has been buying ads through Instacart for the last two years and doesn’t expect the media buying process to immediately change.

“I just see it as expanding the footprint of reach and targeting from home delivery to prepared meal home delivery to grocery delivery, and understanding the data and the connections across those two types of audiences and retargeting across them,” Paternoster said.

U.S. retail media spend is expected to reach nearly $70 billion by the end of this year. Between DoorDash and Instacart’s audience size and potential for closed loop reporting, the case could be made for premium pricing.

“There’s so much data within this that is actually really valuable for advertisers because it spans like every retailer,” said Briana Finelli, vp of brand and commerce media at Goodway Group.

What CPMs could will be is unknown, but advertisers are cautious to earmark ad dollars in advance given new ad programs can take a bit of time to get off the ground. Case in point: OpenAI’s ads in ChatGPT.

Walmart and Amazon are expected to hoover up more than 89% of the incremental ad dollars spent in retail media this year, per eMarketer. The deal between DoorDash and Instacart could help the two delivery companies cut into that spend.

Both DoorDash and Instacart are billion-dollar businesses independently, per reporting from AdExchanger. Walmart’s global ad business pulled in nearly $6.4 billion last year, per its earnings. Those figures, however, pale in comparison to the $21.3 billion Amazon made in Q4 of 2025 alone.

“Amazon and Walmart already have a substantial head start, and the longer it takes to capitalize on that combined data, the wider that gap could become,” said Hengel.

For smaller RMNs, they’ll have more to prove to capture more ad spend.

“Either the ability to prove value in the media itself through transaction level performance data, or the ability to unify and connect their audiences to a larger scale ecosystem, is going to be more important in the coming years,” said Greg Carlucci, senior director analyst at Gartner.

Media buyers and industry analysts seem confident that this deal represents the first major step in retail media consolidation. Increasingly, advertisers are scrutinizing their media spend. For retailers to compete for ad dollars, consolidation can help build up scale, technology and inventory offerings.

“This acquisition is an example point of the importance of scale and measurement that is in commerce media right now,” Carlucci said.

The deal puts an additional spotlight on how RMNs will win over ad dollars. In this instance, the reach will be across “grocery, restaurants, and retail,” said Lidiane Jones, CEO of Integral Ad Science (IAS).

Measurement and incrementality is something the retail media segments have long since grappled with. The acquisition may mean advertisers have access to more data, but access doesn’t guarantee better measurement.

Notably, DoorDash in September received the Alliance for Audited Media’s (AAM) Incrementality Certification, which is an independent auditing program that verifies whether a platform’s incrementality measurement approach aligns with industry standards. Meanwhile, Instacart has partnered with the Interactive Advertising Bureau (IAB) to study measurement in addition to its Media Rating Council accreditation that goes back to 2024.

Still, the industry has yet to settle on a standard definition of incrementality, making it hard to prove even with more data. Meaning, independent verification to ensure retailers aren’t grading their own homework still matters.

“As the category grows, it will be important to pair that scale with consistent, independent evidence of media quality and business outcomes,” said Jones. “The platforms that make that connection will be better positioned to earn sustained investment.”

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

The DoorDash-Instacart deal could accelerate commerce media consolidation, promising scale but risking higher prices and lock-in.

#7230. Good ads have a new name, ‘entertainment-first’ and SharkNinja is using it for its holiday campaign

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Entertainment-first has spent the year working its way into most conversations about marketing, including a fair few of Digiday’s. SharkNinja’s holiday campaign is the latest example.

Last year’s spot had Kevin Hart moving in next door to David Beckham. This year he’s house-sitting for him and he invites Tom Brady and Aryna Sabalenka over. Things get broken an each star then posted a video denying it was them.

The evidence started leaking on social media over the weekend as grainy security camera footage. In the the full spot, out today, Sabalenka drinks straight from the spout of a Ninja slushie machine. Brady wonders about one of the company’s skincare face masks and treads on a present, while Hart cracks a picture frame with a Shark vacuum. Beckham watches the whole thing on his phone live from his own security cameras.

Asked what makes that entertainment-first, Laura Dyer, senior vp of global brand and partnerships at SharkNinja, said it comes down to whether people want to share the ad and talk about it. Anything else is “educational”. That’s a test most memorable advertising has always passed. The fact that marketers now feel obliged to give it a name says something about where the industry has ended up. After years of ads built to satisfy a performance dashboard, making something people would choose to watch has become a position a brand has to declare.

SharkNinja has run this play before. When Brady joined last October, a teaser had the internet expecting a second Netflix roast before the reveal: he was roasting a chicken in a Ninja air fryer. The holiday campaign runs the same trick with a bigger cast and a Christmas tree.

Leaked “CCTV” clips and denial videos from the stars started going up on their accounts and SharkNinja’s at the start of the month two days before the full spot aired today.

“If you make it too long people lose a little interest,” said Dyer on the decision to make the ad’s rollout short. The point is to get people guessing, she continued. “We want people to start speculating, asking ‘what is this from SharkNinja? Where are they? Why are they denying being in David Beckham’s house?’,” Dyer added. Once the spot drops it goes to TV while local brand ambassadors in Italy, France, Germany and other markets will run their own versions under the same “there’s no home like a SharkNinja home” banner, with extensions running through to the end of the year.

The trickier part is that this is still an ad for appliances however it’s dressed up. Somebody still has to tell shoppers what the vacuum does and why it’s worth the money, and the holiday shopping season is when that matters most for businesses like SharkNinja. Dyer and her team tucked those answers into the ads themselves. For instance, Hart vacuums Beckham’s house with the machine bent in the middle so it reaches under a table, a SharkNinja party trick.

“The product is always the hero,” said Dyer. Indeed, the campaign will be judged on sales as much as brand equity so those details matter. Anyone who clicks through from the ad lands on a homepage selling Hart, Beckham and Sabalenka’s favorites.

For a brand that has spent the year being held up as a creator marketing case study, putting Beckham, Hart and Brady at the top of its holiday push is an unexpected call. Dyer called it a matter of fit. “I wouldn’t say it’s conscious at the exclusion of content creators,” she said. The creators haven’t gone anywhere either. They’ll be seeded with the campaign through the season and their videos of kitchens crammed with SharkNinja kit gave the team the idea in the first place.

SharkNinja made the spot without an agency. Its in-house team Creative Studios wrote and produced it while also working directly with each celebrity’s team. The in-house team also made last year’s neighbourhood spot, though that one was done in close partnership with HartBeat — Hart’s own production company. This time SharkNinja did “pretty much all of it by ourselves” Dyer said. That isn’t necessarily a sign of things to come, to be clear. If an agency makes sense, SharkNinja will hire one, she continued, and if it doesn’t the company is happy to do it itself because “our team is talented,” Dyer added.

Beneath the celebs, social media rollout and creators is an ambition the ad business has chased since the jingle.

Dyer described the ideal reaction as people turning to reach others and saying, “this is so funny, you should watch it.” Nobody had to brand that strategy when there were three channels and the ad break was the only way back to the programme. Audiences now skip, scroll and pay extra to get rid of ads so a brand making something people might watch on purpose has to say so out loud. Calling it entertainment-first is mostly an admission of how much of the rest people have learned to ignore. The point rang throughout Advertising Week New York last month.

“Oftentimes we are incredibly comfortable in our industry to waste quadrillions of dollars on things that fake reports justify, though everyone in this room knows it is garbage, “said Gary Vaynerchuk, CEO and chairman at VaynerX. “We are petrified to spend some pennies on something that everyone in this room knows is happening and is real, yet we don’t have the corporate reporting internally yet to justify what’s actually happening.”

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

The DoorDash-Instacart deal could accelerate commerce media consolidation, promising scale but risking higher prices and lock-in.

#7229. As Publicis closes LiveRamp deal, some brands say they’re jumping ship

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Liquid Death confirmed it plans to move away from the platform, which follows ad-holding companies shifting off the service due to the acquisition.

Publicis Groupe closed its $2.2 billion acquisition of data-collaboration platform LiveRamp on Monday, according to a press release. The finalization of the deal follows the companies agreeing to a consent order with the Federal Trade Commission last month which seeks to preserve LiveRamp’s neutrality. Even with those regulatory guardrails in place, some brand marketers appear to be jumping ship.

“You should be worried Publicis Groupe and LiveRamp. Yes we will leave. I am first in line,” Benoit Vatere, chief media and digital commerce officer at Liquid Death, posted on LinkedIn following the news. “Who in their right mind will let an holdco own your data and graph [sic]. Do you really think we are that stupid? All Liveramp competitors, tag yourself in the comment, business is coming your way.”

Vatere’s comment section was flooded with people promoting LiveRamp alternatives. A representative for Liquid Death verified that the beverage brand plans to move away from LiveRamp. Direct Publicis competitors in the agency space, including WPP and Omnicom, have signaled their intentions to wind down use of LiveRamp for months. Publicis did not immediately respond to a request for comment on potential brand marketer defections.

Publicis has positioned the deal, which was first announced in May, as a key piece of its strategy around artificial intelligence, including the ability to develop more sophisticated agentic frameworks on behalf of clients. The release shared more details of how the integration, which pairs LiveRamp tech with Epsilon data, will work to provide a plug-and-play option for bringing together and activating different data sets.

Publicis CEO Arthur Sadoun and Chief Strategy Officer Carla Serrano also appear in a video explaining the benefits of the LiveRamp acquisition, stating it provides a third option for clients who are contending with either walled gardens that provide scale but take away control or the need to hire consultants to develop custom solutions that are time-intensive and costly.

“Our answer is the industry’s first AI growth engine, combining the speed, simplicity and instant results of plug and play, with full ownership and control, so clients can drive business impact without giving up autonomy over their data, their technology, or their future,” said Sadoun in a statement.

Clients can enhance their own first-party data sets with capabilities from LiveRamp’s RampID and Epsilon’s Identity offering. This will allow them to add more customer attributes and promises to make billions of more prospects addressable, the companies claim. The souped-up data sets can also be activated across LiveRamp’s ecosystem, which encompasses a wide range of publishers, tech platforms, walled gardens, retail media networks and large-language models — including OpenAI’s ChatGPT — as well as Publicis’ own media network.

Clients at the same time will not need to navigate the same level of legal processes, custom integration work or security considerations needed to broker partnerships with third-party platforms since Publicis and LiveRamp already have those relationships “pre-wired,” the release said. Publicis and LiveRamp reaffirmed that clients will maintain full ownership and control of their data and intellectual property and will not be locked into using specific technology partners.

#7226. Case for and against – sticking with a Publicis-owned LiveRamp

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

In the months since Publicis Groupe announced its $2.2 billion deal to acquire LiveRamp, CMOs have been considering a crucial question: should I stay, or should I go? That deal closed this week (Monday Nov. 2), bringing the decision to the top of many marketers’ to-do lists.

To recap, clients and their agencies use LiveRamp to onboard their first party data and match it against a large dataset of audience profiles for media targeting and measurement. LiveRamp’s alternative ID solution, RampID, is one of the most commonly used by the market.

But the company’s acquisition by Publicis has prompted questions about the neutrality of this critical digital media infrastructure — and has brought older arguments over the usage of identity solutions back to the fore.

“It’s brought a lot more focus in the industry, both on the client side and certainly in agency land, on the importance and value of these foundational components,” said Jonathan Margulies, managing partner at Winterberry Group.

Here’s the case for and against clients sticking or twisting with LiveRamp.

Upon the deal’s closure, Publicis released a video in which CEO Arthur Sadoun and chief strategy officer Carla Serrano spent six minutes laying out Publicis’ LiveRamp pitch — and attempting to defuse concerns over neutrality and independence of the adtech company’s services.

For Sadoun and Serrano, the pairing’s chief benefit to clients was its “plug and play” nature — the idea that, in using LiveRamp and Epsilon, either as a client of the broader Publicis constellation or not, clients can save upfront investment and act faster. “Clients are trapped between two bad options: surrender their data to the walled gardens for easy scale and lose control, or hire an army of consultants and lose time and a lot of money,” said Sadoun.

Clients that do decide to unplug LiveRamp from their marketing stack will find the search for a new partner an expensive and time-consuming process. LiveRamp is considered to be a market leader, with a huge dataset owing to its larger customer base and partnership deals with commerce networks.

Combining its capabilities with Epsilon makes its position even stronger. Given the IAB projects commerce media spending to grow 13.6% this year, making it the third-fastest growing area of advertising investment after social media and CTV — those capabilities, which are used on the supply-side by the likes of Walmart, Target, Albertsons and Kroger may become more important to CMOs in coming years. LiveRamp is also well-positioned for advertisers hoping to move early into emerging channels like ChatGPT; the firm became OpenAI’s first conversion API partner in June.

For many clients, those arguments will outweigh neutrality worries expressed by Publicis’ holdco rivals (which aren’t, in any case, united in their opposition. Havas, for example, seems set to continue using LiveRamp’s tech in its flagship Converged data platform in North America.)

Furthermore advertisers can now rely upon FTC-enforced rules of the road. Publicis is required to maintain access to LiveRamp by competitors, to set up firewalls to ensure it doesn’t access customer data or confidential business information, and to permit an independent auditor to probe its compliance every six months for two years, and then annually for three years (it doesn’t, however, prohibit the firm from developing integrated products that bridge LiveRamp and its other business units). “It will stay open to every brand, every platform, and yes, every agency,” said Serrano.

While it’s unusual to find companies that commonly spar over new business pitches clients of each other, not every holdco chief is queasy about the prospect. Yannick Bolloré, CEO of Havas, said earlier this year he expected his group to stick with LiveRamp.

“We’re clients of Publicis now,” he told Adweek in July. “We still work with them. Our strategy is to partner with the best data company to offer the right data sets for clients, whether it is LiveRamp, or Epsilon, or Acxiom, or Kantar, or whatever.” If Bolloré can put up with it, can’t you?

Publicis’ ambitions for LiveRamp are tied up in earlier investments in companies like Lotame, Epsilon and Sapient, through which it wants to position itself as a one-stop shop for the entire media investment process. For advertisers already working with its agencies the addition of LiveRamp into the mix is a plus. But for clients that don’t work with Publicis, the neutral status of firms like LiveRamp isn’t a minor issue.

Some CMOs are actively looking for an alternative destination. As Benoit Vatere, chief media officer at Liquid Death, put it in a Monday Linkedin post: “Who in their right mind will let a holdco own your data and graph. Do you really think we are that stupid?”

Agency discomfort over their rivals owning critical infrastructure is more than just a feeling. In 2018 IPG acquired Axciom’s marketing services business, leaving its identity resolution business — LiveRamp — as a separate entity, its independence preserved. Publicis’s 2026 deal has prompted its peers WPP and Omnicom to back off using the platform because they feel that independence has been compromised.

“I don’t see there is any way that you can get any value keeping LiveRamp independent of the rest of your infrastructure,” said John Wren, the CEO of Omnicom, back in May. Stagwell CEO Mark Penn has made similar noises. “They don’t want to indirectly provide a competitive advantage to one of their direct [rivals],” said Margulies.

For clients operating outside the United States, the downside is even larger. 95% of LiveRamp’s $800 million annual revenue comes from the U.S. market. The United States accounts for a huge proportion of digital ad spending, but brands also operating across international borders and regulatory domains might consider the neutrality problem too large a bugbear to bother with, given LiveRamp’s limited application elsewhere.

Their withdrawal could, in turn, impact advertisers that don’t work with the big three “operating companies”. If WPP and Omnicom’s client data is no longer present in LiveRamp’s dataset, then its reservoir shrinks – and its ability to link another advertiser’s first party information to the wider market is diminished.

Although Sadoun told analysts during Publicis’ recent earnings call he was unworried by the policy change, Apple’s recent crackdown on identity resolution has highlighted the limitations of relying on deterministic means of targeting audiences.

For those that want them, alternatives are in the offing. Last week WPP boss Cindy Rose was keen to highlight how its data collaboration platform InfoSum offered a means of patching proprietary data and consumer data secure from the meddling of Califoria tech giants — while Omnicom’s Axciom unit has been developing its own identity solution, Real ID, as a replacement for LiveRamp.

Media buyers have gradually begun relying less on third-party audience data in recent years. According to a OpenX study published earlier this year, 40% supply-side-targeted CTV deal spend processed through its platform in 2025 involved third party targeting data, down from 78% three years earlier. Contextual targeting and vector-based solutions have become more popular among brands like Heineken for targeting on the web or on CTV, while fewer advertisers rely outright on first-party data.

“This is driving brands and their partners to do a lot of soul searching of exactly what their strategy is going to be vis-a-vis data,” said Margulies.

Google’s Q3 earnings reveal AI Mode’s impact on search advertising growth, plus ongoing diminishing returns from ad tech.

At DMBS, agency leaders explained why retail media offers new opportunities to harness closed-loop measurement in a way that benefits both upper- and lower-funnel marketing efforts.

Digiday’s executive editor of news Seb Joseph and senior ad tech reporter Ronan Shields joined the Digiday Podcast to hash out ad tech’s Game of Thrones amid the server-side shift.

#7222. Future of TV Briefing: What it costs brands to fund long-form creator series

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of TV Briefing covers the latest in streaming and TV for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Wednesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at the economics behind brands sponsoring creator-led long-form original video series.

A creator-led long-form original video series can cost a brand anywhere from $150,000 to $5 million, according to Cyndi Steele, a former TV executive producer who leads talent management firm G&B Digital Management’s &Beyond Creators’ Studio.

A six- to seven-figure investment may be an eye-popping amount for CFOs wary of buying into creators. But paying that premium begins to make more sense as video platforms like YouTube, Instagram and TikTok have started highlighting serialized shows and as brands treat creators less like media properties and more like creative collaborators.

Fifty-seven percent of brands said that generating content is a goal for their creator campaigns, according to a study by LTK and Northwestern University released last month. Your reaction to that stat may be something like, “Isn’t generating content 100% the point of brands hiring creators for campaigns?” Yeah. But the shift underlying the stat is how brands are looking to squeeze more content out of the content that creators produce beyond social.

Recycling creator content to this extent requires a lot of content. Which is where the long-form series pitch can come in.

Consider a brand-sponsored, creator-led series that spans 10 long-form episodes plus three short-form videos per episode for a total of 40 pieces of content. That can average between $300,000 to $500,000, according to Steele. Again, that’s a pretty penny. But consider the cost for a brand to produce a traditional TV show.

“When I was in unscripted reality TV, let’s say 10 episodes of a 40-minute-long show, the budget for that is usually around like $5 million. That’s only to do literally the physical production, nothing else,” Steele said.

There are different levers a brand can pull on to adjust the costs of a creator-led long-form show. The episode count can be changed, for example. But the primary line items include:

IP ownership is the least settled aspect of the deal, as Digiday recently reported. For starters, it’s subject to how many ownership the creator retains as well as &Beyond’s ownership stake. The studio prefers first to agree to an ownership split with the creator.

“If it’s an original concept that we’re developing as the exclusive development and production partner, we do split ownership with our talent. A lot of times we like to have the talent typically have the majority ownership. So it a lot of times looks like a 60-40 or 70-30 [split],” Steele said.

But then where does the brand’s cut come from? Well, that becomes a re-appraisal of everyone’s equity. But only if the brand opts to pay for IP ownership.

“The way we’ve approached it so far is they’re not owning the IP. But we can set up specifically owned assets within there, and they can have first right of refusal for future seasons,” Steele said.

Then if a show succeeds and there are future seasons, the brand can pick back up the IP ownership conversation.

“From a brand’s perspective, I think a lot of them aren’t ready to fully dive in with a full IP investment at that level because it is something that takes a long time to really come back and to grow. But from our standpoint, it’s fine because we approach it [by] pulling a little bit from the model of how our managers are currently doing brand deals with the talent and then also how we experience doing brand integrations from the traditional side. It’s just a lot more flexibility,” said Steele.

“When a federal agency formally recognizes millions of creator-led businesses, the people who decide where money goes start to treat creators as an asset class.”

$1.3 billion: Roku’s platform revenue in Q3 2026.

2-3%: Percentage share of Netflix’s London-based employees who are being laid off.

#7219. Levi’s claymation holiday campaign celebrates handmade craft amid AI bloat

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“Let’s Get Fuzzy” is centered on a 60-second spot that required four hours of filming for each second of animation.

Levi’s is taking inspiration from a 30-year-old campaign for its holiday effort. “Let’s Get Fuzzy” serves as a call back to the 1995 ad “Clayman,” the brand’s first global commercial and the first full-length spot completely shot in claymation, according to the brand. The brand’s decision to leverage claymation — which is considered to be incredibly labor intensive — could be welcomed by consumers, the majority of whom are growing tired of AI-generated content, particularly in advertising.

The 60-second video opens on a remote cabin in a snow-covered wilderness where friends are enjoying a holiday gathering. The night takes a turn as a ferocious yeti named Gus tries to break into the cabin before successfully running through the wall. Right when all hope seems to be lost, one of the characters throws a red sweater at the yeti in an apparent act of self defense. However, the sweater fits Gus perfectly, stopping his rampage and leading him to join the party as a welcomed guest.

The campaign, which launched two days after Halloween, meshes holiday aesthetics with vibes of the spooky season with its framing and soundtrack choice, akin to cult classics like “Dead Snow.” The video required four hours of filming for each second of animation, and the set and characters were made by hand, according to press details. No AI was used in the filming process, per the brand.

The collection is available via the brand’s website and app, as well as at select retail locations.

“Our Levi’s Let’s Get Fuzzy campaign was an opportunity to experiment with immersive and tactile world-building,” said Kenny Mitchell, global CMO at Levi’s, in press materials. “Anchored by a film created entirely with stop-motion claymation, it’s also the celebration of the craft and human creativity synonymous with the Levi’s brand.”

Generative AI has become an increasingly common tool for marketers as the industry looks to cut costs and meet the demands of an ever-evolving cultural landscape. However, cost savings and quick turnaround times come at a price. Consumers routinely find AI creative as “annoying,” “boring” and often “confusing,” according to a 2024 study by NielsenIQ. Half of consumers prefer brands that don’t use generative AI in advertising and other forward-facing messaging, per a 2026 Gartner study.

Levi’s isn’t the only company looking to use handcrafted creatives this holiday season. E.l.f. and Prada also launched handmade, stop-motion style holiday campaigns. Retail giants Walmart and Target both launched their campaigns before Halloween. The holiday shopping window has continued to get earlier as consumers hunt for the best value amid budgetary pressure.

Parent Levi Strauss & Co. reported a third-quarter revenue of $1.6 billion, a 4% year-over-year increase, according to an earnings release. Michelle Gass, CEO, president and director of Levi Strauss, noted on an earnings call that there has been less brand-building moments this year and that the brand’s back-to-school campaign did not generate the anticipated demand and traffic. The company plans to deepen its marketing investments and place more of a focus on mid-funnel marketing, according to Gass.

#7218. What DoorDash’s Instacart acquisition says about the modern delivery platform pitch

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

These days, delivery platforms have to offer more than just delivery services to brands and retailers.

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.

Over the years, both DoorDash and Instacart have invested heavily in new services like AI assistants, advertising programs and in-store B-to-B solutions. DoorDash, in particular, has been on an expansion streak, acquiring U.K.-based competitor Deliveroo and restaurant reservations platform SevenRooms in 2025. Last year, DoorDash also acquired search ad startup Symbiosys for $175 million to deepen its advertising capabilities.

With the high cost of last-mile delivery, companies like DoorDash and Instacart have expanded well beyond delivering groceries and takeout.

Leah Sallen, U.S. head of CPG and retail strategy at programmatic ad tech platform MiQ, said the growing services and tools DoorDash has come to provide have made it more than just a marketplace for delivery. “Between delivery, grocery shopping, restaurant reservations and advertising, DoorDash is gaining access to more consumer touchpoints and insights into when, where and how people shop,” she said.

Over the past few years, DoorDash has aggressively built out its non-restaurant marketplace, partnering with major grocery, convenience and retail brands like Ulta, Skims and Macy’s. The company also introduced logistics features to solve pain points, like Dasher Returns. On the technology side, DoorDash has rolled out AI-powered tools, such as Ask DoorDash for shopping recommendations, DashBuddy for courier support and DashOS for restaurant customer management. These solutions are meant to create an all-in-one ecosystem for B-to-B infrastructure and logistics streamlining.

Now, the Instacart consolidation will bring DoorDash grocery retailers that the platform currently does not offer its customers, notably Walmart and Sam’s Club. But with that market share also comes challenges in maintaining retail relationships by offering more than just delivery solutions.

Neil Saunders, managing director of GlobalData, said DoorDash’s latest hefty acquisition will require further streamlining to make sense as a long-term investment.

Saunders said Instacart’s operations still largely rely on workers manually picking groceries. He said this is not only labor-intensive, but it’s also harder to scale efficiently as digital grocery orders continue to grow. “Either way, DoorDash should aim to position itself as a technology and infrastructure partner rather than simply a delivery provider,” Saunders said.

Some experts believe DoorDash’s proactive strategy is to simply keep up with Amazon’s delivery offerings. However, retail media networks have also become an important revenue source for delivery platforms in recent years.

At its surface, the DoorDash-Instacart deal is about two massive last-mile companies consolidating. But for advertisers, it could mean a broader disruption in retail loyalty, said Sallen, as the two platforms’ merger brings together deep data on restaurant, grocery and retail shopping.

“We have already seen this play out with TikTok Shop, which fundamentally changed consumer shopping behavior by using sophisticated algorithms to deliver highly personalized product recommendations,” Sallen said. A similar shift can happen with this acquisition, Sallen said, thanks to the combination of DoorDash’s immediate-consumption insights and Instacart’s long-standing grocery data.

Sallen explained that, similar to how Amazon became a starting point for shopping, consumers may increasingly turn to platforms like DoorDash to find the best product, price or delivery option before deciding which retailer to purchase from. In turn, she said, “That shift from selecting the retailer first to selecting the retailer last could prompt CPG brands to reconsider how they allocate their retail media dollars.”

Matt Gilbert, CEO of marketing platform Partnerize, agreed that the significance of a DoorDash and Instacart combination goes well beyond delivery.

“It’s about the growing concentration of consumer discovery, advertising, purchasing decisions and transaction data within a single commerce ecosystem,” Gilbert said. For consumers, that could mean greater convenience and fewer steps between discovering a product and buying it. “For advertisers, it creates an opportunity to connect marketing investment more directly to purchasing behavior across more categories and occasions.”

On the other hand, Gilbert said that when platforms increasingly control the consumer experience, the transaction and the reporting of advertising performance, advertisers need assurance that they’re measuring genuine incremental influence — “and not simply taking credit for purchases that would have happened anyway,” said Gilbert.

Sallen added that, as platforms like DoorDash add capabilities and AI increasingly influences shopping decisions, “The consumer path to purchase is becoming more diluted and harder for advertisers to follow.”

That shift could become even more pronounced as AI agents increasingly help consumers weigh price, proximity and product availability before deciding what and where to buy. Sallen said this could mean delivery behemoths like DoorDash could have an advantage in shaping AI-driven recommendations to millions of consumers. “The more intelligence it [DoorDash] can bring together, the more personalized it can make the shopping experience,” Sallen said.

“DoorDash’s growing portfolio of services could position it as a one-stop shop for retailers, with data becoming one of its biggest advantages,” Sallen added.

The New York Times, People Inc., News Corp and Reuters report revenue growth despite AI disruption, but face tougher comparisons ahead.

Publishers are pushing back against Meta’s AI scraping terms, testing their leverage over a platform they still rely on.

Political and news creators are relied on now more than ever, but what platforms are working for them? Which ones aren’t?

#7216. Digiday+ Research 2026 Creator Marketing Index: A brand guide to social media campaigns

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This research is based on unique data collected from our proprietary audience of publisher, agency, brand and tech insiders. It’s available to Digiday+ members. More from the series →

As creator marketing becomes a fixture in many brands’ marketing strategies, creators’ roles are shifting and expanding. Brands may pay creators to produce sponsored content, gift products to them in the hope of organic posts, or rely on a combination of creator- and brand-produced content published on their own social media accounts.

Overall, social media remains the leading channel for creator partnerships: 96% of respondents to Digiday’s first-quarter survey of brands and agencies said they partner with creators to create social media content. Within social media, Instagram (90%), Facebook (80%), YouTube (72%) and TikTok (63%) were the platforms marketers said they used most.

Across these social platforms, brands are taking various approaches to creator campaigns, from sponsored and gifted posts to content formats like product demonstrations, styled photos and event coverage. Understanding how these approaches perform can help brands make informed decisions about creator partnerships, content formats and campaign investment.

To measure the impact creator content has on brand campaigns, Digiday+ Research analyzed the engagement levels on leading creators’ social media campaigns from January to September 2026.

The Digiday Creator Marketing Index collected data from 1,755 social media posts and 20 branded social media campaigns, analyzing the top posts associated with each campaign’s hashtag and scoring them across a set of key dimensions to create a total index average score. Results are dependent upon the list of social media campaigns and the time period of data collection, generating a snapshot of the creator marketing landscape at a specific moment in time.

Digiday’s Index collected social media data from January to September 2026. Due to data restrictions, TikTok views were not captured. Data collection was conducted by Dash Social, an agentic social platform powered by brand-intelligent AI.

The social media campaigns were selected by Digiday’s editorial team.

When considering video content performance by platform, Instagram Reels received far more video views than YouTube’s Shorts and long-form videos, while TikTok videos had higher average engagement in later analysis. However, these results should be considered within the context of the Index’s focus on branded social media campaigns, meaning that the majority of posts included marketing-focused content rather than non-brand-related content, which performs the best on YouTube. Additionally, on YouTube — a video-first platform with a core strength in long-form content — most of the captured posts consisted of Shorts rather than long-form posts.

Average engagement rate — engagement divided by follower count — was only measurable on Instagram. When looking at average engagement rate by format type on Instagram, Reels performed the best among Instagram’s formats at an 11% engagement rate, underscoring the strength of short-form video on Instagram. Still posts came in second with a 7% engagement rate, followed by Carousel posts at 5%. Carousel posts can contain videos, but views were not counted as part of the average engagement rate for Carousel posts with video. Likewise, views were not counted as part of the average engagement rate for Still posts or Reels.

The top 75 posts associated with each campaign’s hashtag were identified based on overall engagement, including likes and comments across platforms. When Digiday analyzed those top-performing posts by platform, with engagement weighted equally across platforms, Instagram accounted for the largest share of posts associated with campaign hashtags.

However, TikTok posts within the top 75 generated higher average engagement than posts on other platforms. In other words, Instagram appeared more frequently among the highest-performing posts overall, while the top-performing TikTok posts generated greater engagement on average.

According to Digiday’s analysis, Instagram is a more consistent platform for performance, while TikTok has greater reach potential and a higher engagement ceiling. For brands running longer campaigns or prioritizing consistency, Instagram offers a more predictable environment for creator marketing. For smaller brands or those with limited budgets, TikTok is likely the better platform choice for creator marketing. While engagement on TikTok isn’t as consistent, its higher engagement ceiling gives creator content a better possibility of reaching a wider audience.

Looking more closely at sponsored and gifted posts, the Index defines them as follows. Sponsored posts refer to content created through a direct partnership between a brand and a creator. Gifted posts feature products that brands have sent to creators, typically as part of a PR effort, without a paid content agreement. Digiday classified posts as sponsored or gifted based on disclosures in captions, including paid labels, disclaimers, and hashtags such as #ad, #paid, #gifted and #PR. In some video posts, creators verbally expressed whether they had been paid by a brand.

The most common content types across both sponsored posts and gifted posts were product demonstrations and style shots. The Index defines a demo as content about a single brand or product that includes a description and shows a person using the item. A style shot is content that showcases a product in a scenic or visually styled setting without an explanation of how to use the item.

Style shots appeared more frequently in gifted posts than they did in sponsored posts, by a ratio of 19 to 15, indicating that creators favor that format when featuring products they have received for free without a formal content agreement. One possible explanation is that demos can appear to be overtly promotional since they feature one product as opposed to a “get ready with me” (GRWM) format that typically showcases multiple brands. Creators may want to avoid producing content that could be interpreted as advertising when it comes to gifted items, according to Digiday’s analysis.

When comparing average engagement rates, sponsored posts outperformed gifted posts across content types, with one notable exception: demos  — the most used content format type — where gifted posts performed best. Style shots displayed the opposite pattern, performing better in sponsored content where they also delivered the highest overall engagement among the content formats analyzed.

One potential factor behind this difference is the level of production and associated costs behind the content. Gifted style shots were often simple flat lays or product images, while sponsored style shots frequently included product videos. Brands often give creators larger budgets to develop content for sponsored product posts. That distinction is particularly important for product categories like fragrance, where brands rely on visual storytelling to communicate abstract qualities like mood and sensory experience.

While engagement provides a strong measure of how audiences respond to creator content, reach — calculated by average views — offers another important metric of performance. Digiday’s analysis found that sponsored posts had greater overall reach based on views than gifted posts, by a margin of 334,055 to 48,008. Within sponsored posts, style shots (707,646), demos (250,764) and influencer events (231,595) had the most views. Among gifted posts, demos generated the most views (81,023), well below sponsored formats.

That margin of difference is likely also linked to budget differences, according to Digiday’s analysis. Brands typically provide creators with paid media budgets to amplify sponsored posts beyond a creator’s existing audience, while gifted posts are limited to a creator’s own audience. As a result, sponsored content can generate both organic and paid reach, while gifted product content relies on organic distribution with more limited reach.

This Index exclusively analyzes brand-generated hashtags that incorporated either a brand name or a trademarked product name, such as #exaggereyes, #chocolatesoleil and #hydroboost.

The majority of the highest-ranked campaigns had a strong social media presence with a significant number of brand posts on the brand-owned handle, in addition to creator posts. By contrast, lower-ranked campaigns had a weaker presence on brand-owned channels, with no brand-generated posts in many cases. These findings suggest that while creator participation is an important driver of campaign success, brands’ own social media activity also plays a critical role, according to Digiday’s analysis.

Many of the highest-ranked campaigns also included a mix of gifted posts and sponsored posts. Gifted posts came from both macro- and micro-creators, indicating that high-ranked campaigns sent products to a wide range of content creators. However, investment was another important factor. All of the highest-ranked campaigns had large budgets backing them, highlighting the growing role of pay-to-play social media campaigns, where brands must invest in both creator partnerships and amplification to build reach.

Not all campaigns with paid support performed equally, however, suggesting that investment alone does not guarantee stronger performance.

Charlotte Tilbury’s #exaggereyes campaign ranked No. 1 in Digiday’s Index. One of its defining characteristics was the use of gifted posts: 35% of the campaign’s posts came from creators who received gifted products, the highest share among the campaigns Digiday analyzed.

Charlotte Tilbury’s collaboration with Influenster, a product sampling and review platform owned by Bazaarvoice, generated a large portion of the gifted posts for the #exaggereyes campaign. Rather than selecting creators and sending them PR packages directly, the brand relied on Influenster to collect user-generated content for its own use. Most of the gifted posts were demo videos, a format that Digiday’s analysis found generated higher average engagement within gifted posts than sponsored posts.

Neutrogena’s #hydroboost campaign ranked No. 2 in the Index and focused on a product-line extension. Neutrogena heavily invested in sending gifted products and directly collaborating with creators. As a result, the Hydro Boost campaign had the highest share of brand-seeded content — sponsored or gifted posts — at 39% of all posts.

The campaign also incorporated the broader #hyaluronicacid hashtag alongside the branded #hyrdoboost hashtag. This strategy allowed Neutrogena to not only position the Hydro Boost product as a line extension but also connect the campaign to the larger hyaluronic acid product category, potentially exposing the campaign to audiences with a broader interest in hyaluronic acid products.

Like Neutrogena’s campaign, Too Faced’s #chocolatesoleil campaign promoted a product-line extension but with a focus on the Brazilian market. Thirty-four of the top 75 posts associated with the Chocolate Soleil campaign hashtag mentioned Brazil, including content from Too Faced’s Brazilian social media account and other Brazilian creator accounts, indicating a deliberate focus on localizing the campaign for that market.

The campaign also had the lowest share of sponsored or gifted posts among the 10 highest-ranked campaigns, with 74 of its top 75 posts classified as organic posts. Instead, the campaign focused heavily on the product launch and activity generated through Too Faced’s brand handle to entice creators to test the product and create content about it.

Taken together, these campaigns show that there is no single formula for a successful brand-generated hashtag campaign. High-performing campaigns combined product seeding, creator partnerships, brand-owned content, organic activity and localized strategies in different ways. According to Digiday’s analysis, brand-generated hashtags can give brands greater ownership of the campaign conversation, particularly when tied to a specific product or launch, but their narrower focus may limit the volume and variety of content generated around them.

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

The DoorDash-Instacart deal could accelerate commerce media consolidation, promising scale but risking higher prices and lock-in.
